ewawa: a method of divina- tion practised by the Osm[ [. ] priests: small images of human beings and animals, cowrsies, chalk, charcoal, and a model of a canoe are put on a drum. A chewed kolanut is spat on to it, whereupon the images are put into a cup and thrown on the drum again. The resulting ar- rangements of images are then analysed. If e.Q. the image of a sick man falls into the ‘ canoe", somebody will die. The experts for the feather- and sasswood-ordeals have no special name besides that of ↄb-ita. The witch-doctor, »3ↄbo n-ↄwa n-azε [.......] "" doctor who gives food to the witches", also called sbo n-ↄy-ada [....] "doctor who goes to the cross-roads", leads negotiations with witches and pacifies them by gifts of food from his clients. The "curing doctor"', ↄb-odi [..], bears no outward sign of his profession, but is usually very competent and cures people for money or for their services. A doctor who makes his diagnosis by looking into his patients eyes (in case of illness or ‘ juju-trouble") is called abo n-ↄmi-aɽo [....] i.c. “a doctor who looks eye". ogwsga: practises only that sort of oracle. Other doctors may know the method, but they are not called oↄb-ogwε ga. I[f another doctor does not know the ogweεga, he may have to call in an ↄb-ogwe8ga. The ↄb-ogweεga is paid for his work, but he is also a farmer, as the money he earns is not a sufficient liveli- hood. The oracle is learned from another ogwega doctor (without staying with him); itie [..] (the code) is said to take at least three months to learn. After this, eria [..], the analysis of the code-words, is learnt. That is sald to take mpore than six months. Ewawa doctors are payed with money and they only farm when they have a big family. They make many charms, e.g. some for traders ensuring good business, some warding off danger for travellers, some against witches for sick people, etc. Especially  X 58  “c  a charm 'mentioning a man's name (eb-usuenil....] "name- mentioning charm"). and a charm speaking by itself (ovi_=ɽi.
Bi: "son of the Under- world") procure them more clients than the other oracle doctors have. Oloki: priest, i.e. not a priest at a public shrine, but he has a bigger Olokii shrine in his house than an ordinary Olokii shrine. Many of his clients come to his Oloki shrine and bring things for the yearly Oloki sacrifice (eh-oloku [....]). The oracle method is learnt in about six months" time. During this time the pupil keeps his akpεlε [...]..in. a pot at his OIloki shrine (which nearly every adult possesses). Charms are also put into the pot, and the akp&εlg has first been charmed by the teacher, a full communal Olokii priest. Afterwards the akpéεlεf Is put on an ukpab> [...] tray and taken to some cross-roads (ada [..]) where it is charmed again and buried with the charm for a fortnight "in order to see the truth". (Ada being a meet- ing place for witches, spirits, etc., anything hidden .here sees theni.) Akpεl? and Ogwεga have to be familiar with witches be- cause they procure food for the witches by indicating pacifica-  tion sacrifices, ese [..]. After the fortnight, the pupil, having learnt his craft, takes it out and is an ↄb-akpε]le. The ↄb-ↄrↄmila must be a “priest of J;Imila [...J, i.e. Ifa (Yoruba). The pupil and his family smoothe the kernels by means of a grindstone, wash them with a charm and keep them for three months in a pot of oil. This is called ovi-ↄf3mila [....] "he has taken the ORSmila" (pl verb). During this time small sacrifices are made to them unt:il the pupil is (financially) able to “take" them. uhuki: an influence or power (spirit?) which causes people to do things that are to their dis- advantage, and seem to be senseless. Uhuki is said to be caused by ill-treatment of, or lack of consideration for, one's wife or husband in the preceding reincarnation. The actual cause seems to be the "swearing"  200  uttered during one's previous re- incarnation by the injured party against the offender. Offences leading to this swearing and consequent uhuki are e.g. a man's not giving his wife food and clothes, or not having inter- course with her on the day when she purifies herself after men- struation, or a wife's promiscuous way of living (so that the husband forsakes her), or her omitting to mourn for her de- ceased husband properly (v. xiε [.]); uhuki ↄkpokp-εe [......] "uwhuki (it is that) is troubling him (or her)". The "trouble" caused by uhuki may manifest itself in very different ways, and In different degrees of intensity. A man may refuse to marry and may hate women, or even be temporarily impotent or mad; with a woman it is believed to be the cause of frigidity and cer- taln misdemeanours. A sacrifice can "'bring them (i.e. the uhukis) to sense": a "doctor" makes an Image representing the hus- band or wife, respectively, of the preceding reincarnation, and makes a sacrifice to it. After- wards, the image is buried with the sacrifice (ↄɽe.uhuki [.....] he buried the uhuki). The uhuki was said not to be the dead man (or woman) himself.

(e)v- x (¢) a preposition indicating rest in a locality or motion from a locality; its meaning is often shown more precisely by a following noun of locality, as  2I16  e.g. In v-uwu [..] "in inside" in v-uhudu [..] "at head": on top of; v-otↄ [.] "in ground": underneath. In this way, the English prepositions are ex- pressed by means of one Bini preposition only, linked with various local nouns. v-obↄ [.] lit. "in hand", means "from" when a human being is qualified by it. ov- is also employed to form a great number of ad- verbial expressions, as e.g. v-eva L.O] there; v-odↄ [..] there (a locality still further away than that indicated by v-eva); v-eῦa [.] here; v-oɽe [.. outside; on the street (used when the speaker is at home and means another place in the town).  The " prepositions' formed by.  means of linking v- to a noun of place can also be used ad- verbially, e.g. v-od-aɽo [..] in front (Reneralised by adding odε “road"), in front of; v-iyeke [...] at the back; behind; v-od-uxudu [...] above. Other adverbs formed with the help of v- are v-ehia [:'] "in all": altogether, e.g. ix-ikp>_igie v- ehia (xiε [.] [.... . sold twenty pounds in all": I sold goods amounting to the value of twenty pounds. v-eriↄ [..]
or: or IN. " thus" ; v-gxoxXo [...] "in the corner": privately. (e)v- 2 as; like (used with nouns); sba v-eb-axuε [..'h..] it is red like a parrot tail-feather. v-enε [..] "as if" (introducing sen- tences) ; asye v-en-ↄmawviε [...--.]  he looks (lit. "is") as if he has |  not slept. To v-enε [..] may be added awe [.] "onef says": aɽu  217  v-en-aw-ↄri_ase [...., ..] he be- haves (lit. ""does") as if he were right.
